What isn't public?#

The rig lists for several of the biggest stages in the world. Nobody has published a projector list or supplier for Kinetic Field since 2019, for Ultra's main stage since 2015, or for Tomorrowland's main stage in any year. The Paris 2024 tower lasers have a second career at Disney and still no published supplier or count. Most of what exists was published by vendors with something to sell, so Kvant and Pangolin turn up partly because those companies write their jobs down.

Cue 11

Laser shows now: cheap diodes, fireworks and what is next

The short version: Between about 2005 and 2014 the laser show moved off water-cooled gas tubes and onto solid-state and then diode sources, and rigs went from a pair of lasers to hundreds. Cities have begun using lasers and drones where fireworks used to go, mostly for reasons of fire, smoke and noise. The rules for buying and running a show laser have barely moved: in the US, anything above 5 mW of visible output still needs an FDA variance, and the regulator keeps a standing import alert on projectors that arrive without one.

How did diode lasers change the price and size of a laser show?#

In 2010, hobbyists got their first affordable high-power blue laser diodes by taking apart Casio data projectors. One hobbyist's account, relayed by a laser safety site, has a "harvester" paying about $800 for a projector and selling its 24 diodes at $46 each.295 The 445 nm diode inside was being made in volume as a light source for video projectors, and volume pushed the price down.122

One projector maker's timeline gives the sequence for show lasers: water-cooled gas lasers through the 1990s; DPSS (diode-pumped solid-state) lasers becoming the default around 2005, with prices dropping sharply; diode projection proving itself between 2010 and 2014; and practical 520 nm green diodes around 2014, which made an all-diode red, green and blue projector possible.296 The maker gives no dollar figures, and nobody has published a clean price-per-watt series for those years. How each of these lasers makes its light is in the lasers chapter.

A krypton gas system for a laser dome cost $100,000 in the 1970s. Seattle's Laser Dome ran one until 2016, then replaced it with a digital system of 10 laser projectors.41 In November 2026 the projector maker Kvant's page for a 10 W club projector carried a price of 8,582 EUR before VAT in its metadata, while the page a visitor sees said "price on request".16

Size and count moved the same way. Pink Floyd's 1994 US tour carried two 50 W copper-vapor laser systems, a gear list that survives as a fan transcript of a trade article.234 In 2007 Roger Waters's tour flew five solid-state lasers totaling over 100 W from cranes.235 By 2026 a single touring projector was rated at 58 W or 60 W, and a residency at Sphere in Las Vegas used 20 of the 58 W units, with a row of 60 W units across the stage front on top of that.245 The Guinness record for the number of lasers in one show went from 314 fixtures in 2017 to 1,100 emission sources in 2025.253252 The rigs and the records are in the stage chapter.

The newest hardware trick is variable divergence: a projector that can widen or narrow its beam during the show. Kvant's BeamBrush units were on the 2024 Super Bowl halftime show and on Coachella stages in 2025, by the account of the company whose control software ran them.243244

Are laser shows replacing fireworks?#

In some cities, for some holidays, and usually for a reason that has nothing to do with lasers being prettier.

City When What replaced the fireworks Reason given
Mexico City New Year's Eve 2018 A concert and laser show on Reforma Avenue Air pollution297
Flagstaff, Arizona July 4, 2023 A free laser show set to music Extreme fire risk298
Boulder, Colorado July 4, 2023 Drones Fire danger298
Tahoe City before 2024 Drones Wildfire risk299
Napa and Salt Lake City July 4, 2024 Drones Not stated299
Nicosia New Year 2026 Drones Children, pets, the elderly, people with autism or PTSD; no debris or fire risk300
Athens New Year 2026 "Quiet" fireworks, lights and drones The same300

Drones took most of those slots, with lasers the smaller share. The health argument is about what a firework leaves in the air: an air-quality researcher told ABC News that fireworks release metal particles that can worsen heart and lung conditions.298 The animal argument is mostly about sound. Wildlife researcher Bill Bateman, on what bothers animals at public displays: "it's less the light and more the noise".299

The criticism is that a quiet show is not the same thing. In Mexico City many of the people who came out said they missed the fireworks.297 Katie Biggers, in Tahoe City, put it this way: "Fireworks are something that just hits all of us."299 A Canadian fireworks trade group, asked about Vancouver's fireworks-free New Year, said drone shows cost more and that fireworks "are felt with the nose, the eyes, the senses".301

Lasers and fireworks also share a bill. The FDA's laser light show page lists use alongside fireworks among the ordinary settings for a laser display.28

Is that beam in the sky a laser or a searchlight?#

If it is a tight colored line that stays pencil-thin for miles, it is probably a laser. If it is a fat white shaft sweeping the clouds over a car dealership, it is probably a lamp.

The lasers are easy to name when an artist signs them. Yvette Mattern's "Global Rainbow" is seven parallel laser beams, one per color of the spectrum, first shown in New York on January 19, 2009 and later in Berlin, Toulouse, Utrecht, Nantes, three UK sites for the London 2012 Cultural Olympiad, New Haven, Pittsburgh, Derry, Leuven, Cleveland and Toronto.302 How far it can be seen depends on who is asked: an art site says "up to 35 miles", and the laser supplier says "more than 60 km" for Nantes in 2011. Neither figure was measured.273303 Chris Levine's "Higher Power" over Venice in May 2026 was a single green 515 nm laser beam, and Rockne Krebs ran one 7 miles from Mount Wilson to Caltech in 1983.27438

A laser carries because it barely spreads. A physics news service worked the example for a 5 mW pointer: at 100 m the spot is about 100 times wider than where it started, and from an aircraft at 40,000 ft in clear air it would still look about as bright as a quarter moon.11

The classic searchlight is a xenon lamp in a reflector. The line between the two is blurring from the lamp side: Claypaky's Skylos, reported in 2023, keeps the searchlight housing and puts a 300 W white laser source inside it. It draws 600 W, is rated IP66, has a beam adjustable from 0.5 to 5 degrees, and the maker claims output equal to a 4,000 W xenon lamp.23

Any beam aimed at the sky is also an aviation matter. US pilots reported 10,994 laser strikes in 2025, down 14 percent from 12,840 in 2024 and the second yearly fall in a row, with 337 pilot injuries reported since tracking began in 2010.224 Those reports are overwhelmingly about handheld pointers, and US law treats them seriously: FAA civil fines of up to $11,000 per violation, and federal criminal penalties of up to five years and $250,000.225 For shows, the FDA lists "distraction hazards for pilots and drivers" among the risks of a badly run display and points producers to the FAA's outdoor laser material; UK guidance from the Health and Safety Executive says outdoor beams should follow Civil Aviation Authority guidance, with a notice to pilots filed.2830 The detail is in the rules chapter.

Are planetarium laser shows still a thing?#

Yes. At the University of Arizona's Flandrau planetarium, every laser show sold out for 18 months, showings went from two to three every other week, and total attendance reached 46,339 in fiscal 2023, up from about 32,000, with the laser shows named as a significant contributor.92 Fiske Planetarium in Boulder marked its 50th year in 2025 with a laser and music show whose classic rock program sold out before its September 19 debut.109 Saint Louis Science Center lists Nutcracker, Trans-Siberian Orchestra and Taylor Swift laser shows for December 2026 at $14.108 Seattle's Laser Dome is still running after about 50 years, even as its parent institution laid off staff in March 2026.100 The one place they are gone is where they started: Laserium left Griffith Observatory in 2002 and never went back.5 The full story, including who makes the shows and who clears the music, is in the planetarium chapter.

Can you buy a laser show projector?#

The published rules differ by country. What follows describes them and is not advice on any particular purchase.

United States (law, with FDA guidance on top). Laser show projectors are demonstration laser products, and without a variance from the FDA they are limited to Class IIIa: 5 mW of visible output. Class IIIb and Class IV show equipment needs variance approval "before sale or use". The paperwork is a Product Report (Form 3632) from the maker, a Laser Light Show Report (Form 3640) and a Variance Application (Form 3147) from the producer, and the FDA's page is blunt about the order of events: "Your firm may begin to produce laser light shows only after receipt of a variance approval letter."28 The agency's guidance to the trade, Laser Notice 51 of May 27, 2001, adds that "projectors may only be delivered to customers having an approved variance", and that even demonstrating a projector to a customer counts as putting on a show.219

Imports into the US. FDA Import Alert 95-04, first issued in December 1997 and revised on July 31, 2026, lets border staff detain, without examining them, laser pointers, light show projectors and similar products from firms on its list. The grounds include output above 5 mW, missing warning labels and no product report on file. The list runs to roughly 120 firms, nearly all of them in China, and one entry for light show projectors carries the note "Lack of variance".220 The FDA's laser light show page also links a safety communication in which the agency recommends against certain named projector models.28

United Kingdom. Consumer laser products fall under the standard BS EN 50689:2021 and may be Class 1, Class 2 or a subgroup of Class 3R. The UK Health Security Agency's advice, updated on March 18, 2025, says Class 3B and Class 4 lasers "are not suitable for general consumer use", and warns that pointers labeled Class 2 have measured as 3B or 4. Shining a laser at vehicles and aircraft is covered by the Laser Misuse (Vehicles) Act 2018.29 For shows, the HSE's guidance describes no license like the US variance. The duty sits with organizers, venues, suppliers and operators under general safety law, and the HSE's older HS(G)95 booklet has been withdrawn in favor of a free guide from the trade body PLASA, which the HSE endorses.30

European Union. The published picture is patchier. In Germany the OStrV regulation requires a written laser safety officer appointment for Class 3R, 3B or 4 lasers unless the employer has the expertise.18 The 2025 record show in Amsterdam was run to the laser safety standard DIN EN 60825-1, with sign-off by outside testers.255

Is it safe to go to a laser show?#

The FDA's summary of a compliant show is that it will "keep hazardous lasers away from the audience"; the risks it lists for badly handled lasers are eye injury, skin burns, fire and the distraction of pilots and drivers.28 Phones are less protected than eyes. ILDA's line is that "Camera sensors are, in general, more susceptible to damage than the human eye."206 On light and sound, at least one planetarium tells people who are sensitive to either not to attend.108 The rest - classes, exposure limits, injuries on the record, camera damage - is in the safety chapter.

How do you become a laserist?#

There is no single door. A laserist or laser artist performs or operates the show, often mixing pre-programmed cues with live control. Seattle's Laser Dome has six staff laser artists who perform live on Pangolin Beyond.41 One of them, Emily Early-Griffith, describes the job as part preparation, part performance: "We have stuff that we've designed and choreographed beforehand."100

The formal training is short and mostly about safety. ILDA runs a Lasershow Operator course of 4 hours plus a 45-minute test, a Laser Safety Officer course for light shows of about 9 hours including a 2-hour test, and audience-scanning training; other providers run one-day and two-day courses. ILDA's 2025 course list gives no prices.18 The same classes, plus laser content creation, are taught at the LDI trade show in Las Vegas, where the content class earns ETCP renewal credits.304

Whether any of it is required depends on the map. Germany requires a written laser safety officer appointment. Arizona requires each registrant to designate one. The US federal variance form does not mention one except where audience scanning is approved. The UK has no formal qualification.18 ILDA's company accreditation asks for an employee who has passed a Laser Safety Officer course.265 The working day of an operator is in the programming chapter.

What is coming next?#

Less than the hardware might suggest, on the regulatory side.

  • US rules: unchanged where shows are concerned. The FDA re-issued Laser Notice 56 on February 21, 2023. It says products that conform to the international standard IEC 60825-1 Ed. 3 will not face enforcement of the matching parts of 21 CFR 1040.10 and 1040.11 - and it expressly leaves out variances and the rules for demonstration laser products. The show variance system stands as it was.221
  • US paperwork: by email. Variance filings now go to the FDA's device center by email, and the separate filing with Dockets Management is no longer required.28
  • US imports: still watched. Import Alert 95-04 was revised on July 31, 2026.220
  • UK consumer advice: rewritten around EN 50689, in the update published on March 18, 2025.29
  • Safety that acts without the operator. The 2025 record show in Amsterdam ran with automatic beam interruption, and the HSE lists scan-failure safety systems, emergency stops and barriers among the standard controls for a display.25530
  • Brighter single units and wider beams. The 58 W and 60 W projectors at Sphere in 2026 and the variable-divergence units on stadium and festival stages are what the trade press has been writing about since 2023.245244
  • Lasers inside things that are not laser shows. The laser-source searchlight is one example.23 The far larger one is the video projector, which is what the multi-billion-dollar "laser projector" market forecasts describe.267

Cue 12

How a laser works, and who invented it

The short version: A laser is a light amplifier with a mirror at each end, and what leaks out of it is a narrow, single-color beam whose waves move in step. Einstein described the effect behind it in 1917, Charles Townes built a microwave version in the 1950s and Theodore Maiman made the first one that worked with light in May 1960. The question of who deserved the credit kept a patent fight going for 30 years.

How does a laser actually work?#

Start with the rule that atoms are fussy about energy. An atom or molecule can hold only certain set amounts, its energy levels, and the lowest is the ground state. When an excited electron drops to a lower level, the difference comes out as light.305 Usually that happens on its own, in a random direction, which is spontaneous emission and is roughly how a light bulb glows.305

The laser trick is stimulated emission: a passing photon with the right energy makes an excited atom release a second photon that matches it in direction, frequency and phase.306 The catch is that in any normal material, more atoms sit in the lower state than the upper one, so a passing photon is more likely to be absorbed than copied.305 To win, you need a population inversion - more atoms up than down - and heat cannot produce one. The atoms have to be selectively excited, or pumped, usually with light or electricity.305307

So every laser has three parts. The gain medium is where the inversion happens: a gas, a crystal, a dye, a fiber or a semiconductor. The pump feeds it energy. The resonant cavity, usually two facing mirrors, sends light back through the medium again and again, and the beam leaves through the partly reflective one, the output coupler.307 The mirrors are what turn an amplifier into an oscillator.305

How the levels are arranged matters. A three-level laser such as ruby has to build its inversion against a full ground state, so it usually pulses; a four-level laser empties its lower level fast and can run a steady beam.305

Why is laser light different from a flashlight?#

Three properties fall out of the mechanism. The light is coherent because each stimulated photon copies the phase, wavelength and direction of the one that triggered it, and the cavity keeps reinforcing that.305 It is close to monochromatic because only wavelengths whose round trip fits the cavity a whole number of times can build up; these are the longitudinal modes, and a laser runs on a few of them within about 0.1% of each other.305 And it is narrow because the cavity confines the light along the mirror axis.

How narrow depends on the laser. A helium-neon beam typically spreads about 1 milliradian, while a semiconductor laser can diverge 20 degrees or more.305

What is the difference between continuous, Q-switched and mode-locked lasers?#

A continuous-wave (CW) laser puts out a steady beam; a pulsed laser fires flashes.308 Two tricks make the pulses short and hard:

  • Q-switching holds lasing back while the gain builds, then releases it as one intense pulse, typically nanoseconds long, at around 10 Hz to 100 kHz.308 Hellwarth proposed it in March 1961, and he and McClung demonstrated it in 1962.309
  • Mode-locking locks the cavity's modes in phase so they add up into a train of picosecond or femtosecond pulses at MHz to GHz rates.308 Hargrove, Fork and Pollack first showed it in a helium-neon laser in 1963.309

What does "laser" stand for, and who came up with the word?#

Laser is short for light amplification by stimulated emission of radiation, and the first recorded use of the acronym is in Gordon Gould's notebook of November 13, 1957, headed "Some rough calculations on the feasibility of a LASER."310309 The word went public at a conference in 1959, and Arthur Schawlow and colleagues resisted it; Bell Labs' term was optical maser.310311

It copied an older acronym. Maser - microwave amplification by stimulated emission of radiation - was named by Townes over lunch in a Columbia cafeteria in April 1954, after he turned down names ending in "-tron."312

What did Einstein have to do with it?#

He supplied the physics 43 years early. In 1917, in a paper on the quantum theory of radiation published in Physikalische Zeitschrift (vol. 18, p. 121), Einstein described three ways light and matter trade energy: absorption, spontaneous emission and stimulated emission, which the surrounding radiation triggers. He used the three to derive Planck's radiation law.313 Nobody put stimulated emission to work until the 1940s and 1950s.306

Who built the maser, and when?#

Townes conceived the idea on April 26, 1951.309 He built it at Columbia with James Gordon and Herbert Zeiger in 1953-1954: an ammonia maser.314 The date it first worked moves with the source. Britannica gives the end of 1953, APS News 1953, Photonics Spectra 1954 and a 2017 Physics World piece 1955.311315309313 The full paper, "The Maser - New Type of Microwave Amplifier, Frequency Standard, and Spectrometer," appeared in Physical Review on August 15, 1955.312314

In Moscow, Basov and Prokhorov at the Lebedev Institute worked out the theory of maser operation independently.311 They proposed a pumping method in 1955, and Bloembergen developed the solid-state maser in 1956.309

What was the 1958 Schawlow and Townes paper?#

It was the blueprint. In 1957 Townes suggested to Schawlow, then at Bell Labs, that they push maser action up to infrared and visible light, and their "optical maser" paper ran in Physical Review on December 15, 1958 (vol. 112, pp. 1940-1949).311315 Schawlow's contribution was putting mirrors at each end of the cavity.306 They published before building anything.306

Bell Labs filed the patent in July or August 1958, depending on the source, and US 2,929,922 was granted on March 22, 1960 - two months before anyone had a working laser.310316309 In September 1959 Schawlow argued in public that pink ruby could not work as a laser.31

What happened with Gordon Gould and the laser patent?#

Gould was a Columbia graduate student when he wrote his nine-page notebook in November 1957 and had it notarized at a neighborhood candy store.317318 Townes had told him to write the ideas down and get them notarized.319 In March 1958 Gould left Columbia without finishing his PhD to join a company called TRG, and in April 1959 Gould and TRG filed patent applications, eight or nine months after Bell Labs.316309

Once the Schawlow-Townes patent issued in 1960, Gould challenged it, starting a dispute that ran 30 years.309 He lost the main priority fight, largely because his notebook did not say outright that the laser's sidewalls were transparent.310 He did better on the patents that followed.

  • 1968: his first laser patent, for generating X-rays with a laser.310
  • 1970: he bought his patent rights back from Control Data, which had acquired TRG. Wikipedia puts the price at $1,000 plus a small share of future profits; the Photonics Spectra timeline says $1 plus 10% of future proceeds.310309
  • 1973: the US Court of Customs and Patent Appeals ruled the Schawlow-Townes patent too general.315
  • 1977: US 4,053,845, on optically pumped laser amplifiers, issued on October 11.309310
  • 1979: US 4,161,436, covering uses including welding, cutting, drilling, distance measurement, communications and laser fusion. Gould and his backers formed Patlex to hold and enforce the patents.310
  • 1985: a federal court ordered the Patent Office to issue his gas-discharge amplifier patent, US 4,704,583.310 An appeal from the same year, Patlex Corp. v. Mossinghoff, lists Patlex and Gould against the Commissioner of Patents.320
  • 1987: Gould prevailed in the struggle over royalties after Patlex won its first decisive enforcement case.319310
  • 1988: his fourth and last basic laser patent was approved in April, and royalties began.318315309 By then, Electronic Design reported, his amplifier patents covered 80% of US-made lasers in a market worth over $500 million a year.318

The money is reported two ways. In 1989 Gould, then 68, expected close to $2 million a year once every licensee signed; Patlex owned 64% of the patent income, Gould 20% and Refac Technology Development Corp. the rest, and many laser makers said the fees were too high.321 Over his lifetime he signed away 80% of the proceeds to pay for the litigation and still earned several million dollars; Innovate Long Island puts the eventual payday at $30 million, an estimate, against about $100,000 had he been granted the patent promptly.310317 He held 48 patents, entered the National Inventors Hall of Fame in 1991 and died in New York on September 16, 2005, aged 85.310318

Who built the first laser that worked?#

Theodore Maiman, with a ruby crystal about 1 by 2 cm. On May 16, 1960, at Hughes Research Laboratories, he and his assistant Irnee d'Haenens drove the spiral flash lamp wrapped around it past 950 V and saw the first sign of laser action.31 The proof was not a bright beam but spectral narrowing above threshold, measured later that month.31322 Townes later wrote that the pulsed lamp emptied the ruby's ground state enough for stimulated emission, which he called unexpected.322 The line sits at 694.3 nm, deep red.323

Then the journals turned him down. Physical Review Letters rejected his report in late June 1960.316 The story usually told is that it was dismissed as one more maser paper in a flood of them; Townes, who called that explanation a common belief, put the decision on editor Simon Pasternack, who judged it "simply more of the same" as a ruby paper Maiman had published that June.31322 Maiman sent a shorter letter, "Stimulated optical radiation in ruby," to Nature, which printed it on August 6, 1960 (vol. 187, p. 493).31324

Hughes had already called a press conference on July 7. The photo it handed out showed a later prototype, not the first laser, papers ran "death ray" angles, and some scientists were skeptical because the Nature paper was not out yet.31322 Bell Labs showed a pencil beam from its own ruby device on August 1 and published in October.31316

Who made the first helium-neon laser?#

Ali Javan, William Bennett Jr. and Donald Herriott at Bell Labs, in December 1960. It was the first gas laser and the first with a continuous beam.311 Javan had published the gas-laser idea in 1959. He saw the beam on December 12; the next day the team carried a telephone call over it, and their letter appeared on January 30, 1961.325

That first beam was invisible, at 1.15 micrometers in the infrared. The red 632.8 nm line that became the classroom laser came a few weeks later from Alan White and Dane Rigden at Bell Labs.326 In between, in November 1960, Sorokin and Stevenson at IBM ran a uranium-doped calcium fluoride laser, the second solid-state laser.309316

When were the first semiconductor lasers made?#

In a three-way race in autumn 1962. At General Electric, Robert Hall's team got gallium arsenide diode L-52 to lase on Sunday, September 16, when Gunther Fenner pulsed it above 12 A; they submitted to Physical Review Letters on September 24 and published on November 1.327 Marshall Nathan's team at IBM published in Applied Physics Letters the same day, and MIT Lincoln Laboratory (Quist, Keyes and Rediker) followed on December 1. An MIT spokesperson told the New York Times that GE got there "a couple days or a week" earlier, and an IEEE Milestone credits all three.328 Those first diodes ran in liquid nitrogen.328

Nick Holonyak at GE Syracuse published a visible red gallium arsenide phosphide diode in October 1962. Continuous room-temperature diodes took until spring 1970, from Alferov's group in the USSR and from Panish and Hayashi at Bell Labs.309

Who invented the CO2 laser?#

Kumar Patel at Bell Labs. He found laser action in carbon dioxide in 1963, getting tens of milliwatts at 10.6 micrometers on the first try; adding nitrogen pushed it to 10 W.329 The nitrogen-CO2 laser of 1964 was, in Lemelson-MIT's description, the first gas laser to produce high power continuously.330 Classified work scaled it fast: Gerry and Kantrowitz at Avco Everett showed a gas-dynamic CO2 laser in 1966, kept classified until 1970, by which time such lasers reportedly reached 100 kW.329 Patel received the National Medal of Science in 1996.330

Who invented the Nd:YAG laser?#

A Bell Labs team in 1964, though the roster varies. SPIE names Geusic, Marcus and Van Uitert, who first ran neodymium-doped yttrium aluminum garnet at 1.06 micrometers; the Photonics Spectra timeline credits "Geusic and Smith."329309 Its 1064 nm line can be doubled to 532 nm green, tripled to 355 nm and quadrupled to 266 nm.323

The rest of the family followed quickly. Sorokin and Lankard at IBM showed liquid dye lasers in 1966, and Bernard Soffer and McFarland made one tunable in March 1967.329309 The first excimer laser, a xenon dimer pumped by an electron beam, ran at the Lebedev Institute in Moscow in 1970, fulfilling a Basov prediction.331

Who called the laser "a solution looking for a problem"?#

Britannica gives the joke to Irnee d'Haenens, Maiman's young colleague.311 Townes wrote that many people, himself included, called the laser that, partly as a joke and partly as a challenge, and named no originator.322 The problems did not take long to arrive: the first medical laser treatment of a human patient was in December 1961, and Leith and Upatnieks at the University of Michigan made the first laser holograms in late 1963.309311

Which Nobel Prizes have been given for lasers?#

Townes and Schawlow each won one; Gould and Maiman did not.

Year Physics laureates For
1964 Charles Townes (1/2); Basov and Prokhorov (1/4 each) quantum electronics leading to oscillators and amplifiers on the maser-laser principle332
1966 Kastler optical pumping methods333
1971 Gabor holography333

1997 Chu, Cohen-Tannoudji, Phillips cooling and trapping atoms with laser light335

2018 Arthur Ashkin (1/2); Gérard Mourou and Donna Strickland (1/4 each) optical tweezers; chirped pulse amplification336

The 2018 prize reaches into everyday life. Chirped pulse amplification stretches a pulse, amplifies it and squeezes it back down, from a 1985 paper by Mourou and Strickland, and the Nobel committee says it is now used in millions of eye operations a year.336 Ashkin's optical tweezers held living bacteria in 1987.336 For the 2023 prize, Krausz had isolated a single 650-attosecond pulse in 2001.337

What are the main kinds of laser?#

Lasers are sorted by gain medium. The wavelengths below are the reference lines, and the last column links to where each family gets used.

Cue 13

Lasers that make things: cutting, welding, engraving and chips

The short version: Most of the laser power on Earth goes into heating things up on purpose - melting a slot through steel plate, fusing a battery busbar, burning a code into a surgical tool or turning tin droplets into plasma to print computer chips. Fiber lasers overtook CO2 lasers for sheet-metal cutting in 2015 and have kept climbing in power, while desktop engravers have put Class 4 lasers on kitchen tables and some of them have been turned away at the border. In factories the usual answer to a multi-kilowatt beam is a box with interlocks around it.

How does a laser cut metal?#

Laser cutting is "mainly a thermal process," and it comes in three flavors.338 In fusion cutting the beam melts the metal and a jet of inert gas, usually nitrogen, blows the melt out of the kerf, the slot the cut leaves. In flame cutting the jet is oxygen, which burns the hot steel and adds heat of its own. In remote cutting a high-intensity beam vaporizes thin sheet outright, with no gas at all.338

The gas choice shows up on the part. Oxygen leaves an oxidized edge. Nitrogen leaves an oxide-free cut that can go straight to paint or welding without cleaning.339

Lasers have been working in manufacturing since the 1960s.338 For most of that time the cutting workhorse was the CO2 laser, a gas laser at about 10.6 µm (the families are compared under invention). Fiber, disk and Nd:YAG lasers are solid-state lasers, running at a much shorter wavelength.338 TWI gives typical laser cut thicknesses of up to 20 mm for carbon-manganese steel, 12 mm for stainless and 10 mm for aluminum.338

Have fiber lasers replaced CO2 lasers for cutting?#

On sales, yes. In 2011 fiber lasers were about 5-10% of cutting laser sales. In 2015 they outsold CO2 for the first time.339 CO2 cutting sales had clustered at 4-5 kW with some 6 kW machines, and CO2 lasers of 6 kW and up had existed since the 1990s. At Fabtech 2014, fiber systems on the floor ran from 2 kW to 12 kW.339

The speed numbers explain the switch. On quarter-inch mild steel with nitrogen, an Amada America author reported in 2016 that a 4 kW CO2 cut at about 60-80 in/min, a 6 kW CO2 at about 110-120 in/min, and a 6 kW fiber at about 200 in/min.339 CO2 still has an edge in thick stock: TWI says it cuts material over 5 mm faster than fiber at the same power.338

CO2 laser Fiber laser
Type Gas laser Solid-state (doped optical fiber)
Wavelength about 10.6 µm -
Typical cutting power 4-5 kW, some 6 kW (reported, 2016) 2-12 kW at Fabtech 2014; 30 kW called mainstream in China (estimate, 2024)
1/4 in mild steel, nitrogen, 6 kW about 110-120 in/min about 200 in/min (reported, 2016)
Thick material (over 5 mm) Faster at equal power Slower at equal power
Share of cutting laser sales Majority until 2015 5-10% in 2011; outsold CO2 from 2015
Operating cost - about $1-3 per hour (reported, 2016)

Sources: TWI338; Laser Focus World, 2016339; Tianxia Gongchang, 2026340.

Power has kept going up, especially in China. A 2026 industry report calls 30 kW the mainstream fiber cutting configuration there in 2024, says Chinese brands shipped about 21,000 units of 10 kW and above that year, and repeats Raycus's claim to the first commercial sale of a 200 kW fiber laser in September 2024.340 All three are estimates or company claims with no named dataset behind them, and the same report gives domestic share of the 10 kW-plus market as over 70% "in 2024" in one place and "projected" for 2025 in another.340

What does a laser weld?#

Batteries and motors, increasingly. In electric vehicles, lasers weld the busbars that join battery cells into modules and packs, in like metals (aluminum to aluminum, copper to copper) or mixed (aluminum to copper). They also seal cells gas-tight, weld battery trays and housings, and weld the hairpin copper bars of e-motor stators, plus rotor shafts, gears and fuel-cell bipolar plates.341

Copper is awkward to weld, and TRUMPF markets a green-wavelength disk laser that it says welds copper contacts "practically without any spatter," without giving the wavelength.341

OSHA's list of industrial laser jobs puts welding alongside cutting, drilling, soldering, heat treating, annealing, scribing, sealing, marking and balancing.342

Why do medical devices carry laser-marked codes?#

Because the FDA wants the code to survive the autoclave. Under 21 CFR 801.45, a device that must carry a Unique Device Identifier (UDI) on its label must also carry a permanent UDI on the device itself if it is meant to be used more than once and reprocessed before each use.343 The mark can be plain text, AIDC (automatic identification and data capture, such as a 2D code) or both. Exceptions cover cases where marking would harm the device's safety or effectiveness, or isn't technologically feasible, and the labeler has to document the reason in its design files.343

The rule names no marking method; a laser is one way to make the mark permanent, and the choice is the manufacturer's.

What are desktop laser engravers, and what class are they?#

They are the reason a Class 4 laser can now ship in a box addressed to an apartment. The category took off around 2021, when the Makeblock spin-out xTool launched its D1 engraver.344

The money got large fast. xTool's Hong Kong listing application went public on January 1, 2026. A write-up of the prospectus reports revenue of RMB 1,456.6 million in 2023, RMB 2,475.9 million in 2024 (up 70%), and RMB 1,776.7 million for the first nine months of 2025 (up 18.6%), with more than 405,000 connected machines deployed as of September 30, 2025.345 KrASIA, reporting from 36Kr in November 2026, put full-year 2025 growth above 30%, first-half 2026 revenue up roughly 80-90%, and annual revenue around RMB 2 billion - a figure that sits below the prospectus's 2024 number.344 A former employee told the outlet the category had become saturated by 2024. Ortur, AtomStack and Hingin are named as earlier, slower-growing entrants, and Bambu Lab's R1 laser cutter starts at US$2,499 against about US$3,000 for comparable xTool products (reported, 2026).344

Glowforge, founded in Seattle in 2015, took $27.9 million in sales in a 30-day crowdfunding campaign that October and raised about $183 million in total.346 In 2025 it restructured through an Assignment for the Benefit of Creditors (ABC), a state-law alternative to bankruptcy. After a sale process drew no bidders, co-founders Dan Shapiro and Mark Gosselin bought the brand, platforms and Seattle manufacturing rights. The Seattle production facility closed in September 2025, and the restarted company supports about 20 former employees, down from a little over 90 early that year.346

The engineering lives in the laser class. In Bambu Lab's safety document for the H2D, the laser module is 10 W ± 1 W, emitting at 455 nm (blue) and 850 nm (infrared), with the 40 W spec listed as "(To be updated)." The module alone is Class 4. The whole machine is Class 1 only "when the printer's protection is complete and properly working."347 That protection is a stack of interlocks on the door, top glass and window, plus module detection, flame and temperature detection and a safety key. The document also tells owners to watch basswood and acrylic for open flames, keep a CO2 extinguisher, never leave the machine running unattended, vent through a 100 mm exhaust and never cut PVC.347 An open-frame engraver has no such box around its beam. How the classes are defined is covered under safety.

What have regulators found?#

In the UK, the Office for Product Safety and Standards (OPSS) has acted on two engraver cases.

In August 2024 it reported on the Creality Falcon2, a 22 W Class 4 laser engraver marketed for consumer use. OPSS found the laser "excessively strong" for non-professional use and found that the supplied goggles did not give the required protection. It rated the risk serious ("damage to sight"), found the product failed the Supply of Machinery (Safety) Regulations 2008 and PPE Regulation (EU) 2016/425, and the import was rejected at the border.348

In October 2022 OPSS recorded a recall of Vevor laser engraving machines (models L3040, XTS05, S3018 Pro and S3018 Pro Max). That finding was electrical, not optical: the plug pins were narrower than the 6.22 mm minimum, creating an electric shock risk, and safety instructions were missing. The risk was rated serious. The machines were recalled from end users and imports were rejected and destroyed.349

In the US, the paperwork changed at the end of 2024 (the wider rules are under regulation). The FDA withdrew Laser Notice No. 50 on December 31, 2024, and manufacturers moved to Laser Notice No. 56, issued May 8, 2019, which lets them certify using parts of IEC 60825-1 Ed. 3 (and IEC 60601-2-22 Ed. 3.1 for medical lasers) in place of some parts of 21 CFR 1040.10 and 1040.11.350 Bambu's H2D certification line cites exactly that notice.347

Can a laser really strip rust and paint?#

Yes, by blowing the top layer off. Laser cleaning fires a very brief, high-power pulse that ablates the surface; part of it vaporizes and part becomes particulate dust a filter can catch.351 TWI describes two modes. In coating removal, paint, rubber or insulation absorbs the beam and the metal underneath is left alone. In surface decontamination the whole top layer comes off - for example radioactive concrete, a job called "scabbling."351

Uses include cleaning electronics contacts and tire molds, and stripping paint from buildings, bridges, aircraft and ships where solvents are restricted. It is non-contact, uses no chemicals and can run remotely down a fiber.351 China's laser cleaning market was about RMB 1.96 billion in 2024, an estimate with no named source.340

How does a laser 3D print metal?#

One micro-thin layer at a time. In laser powder bed fusion (GE calls it DMLM; others say DMLS or SLM), a laser fully melts metal powder into small liquid pools, tracing each layer from a CAD file. Materials include titanium, cobalt-chrome and aluminum alloys.352 Binder jetting, by contrast, glues powder and sinters it in a separate furnace step.352

The poster child is GE's LEAP jet-engine fuel nozzle. GE started developing it in 2004 and bought Morris Technologies and RQM in November 2012. The printed nozzle consolidated about 20 parts into one, came out 25% lighter and had a reported fivefold life improvement. It was built in cobalt-chrome on an EOS M270, with a plan for more than 25,000 parts a year rising to more than 40,000 a year by 2018 (company figures, 2013).353 The same GE engineers listed the catch: subsurface porosity and rough down-facing surfaces of about 1,000 µin Ra, dealt with through stress relief, hot isostatic pressing (HIP) and solution heat treatment.353

What lasers are inside chip making?#

Two very different ones. DUV lithography runs on excimer lasers, ultraviolet gas lasers. Krypton fluoride (KrF) at 248 nm came first; argon fluoride (ArF) at 193 nm enabled features as small as 38 nm.354 EUV lithography uses 13.5 nm light, which ASML describes as more than 14 times shorter than DUV.354

No laser emits at 13.5 nm directly in these machines. A generator fires molten tin droplets about 25 µm across at 70 m/s. A low-intensity pulse flattens each droplet, and a stronger pulse vaporizes it into a plasma that emits EUV, 50,000 times a second.354

The laser doing the hitting is a CO2 laser - the old cutting workhorse, rebuilt. TRUMPF's drive laser takes seed pulses of a few watts, pre-amplifies them to 100 W and then amplifies them to 40 kW, with peak power in the megawatt range, total gain above 10,000x and a beam path of up to 30 m to the droplet chamber. ASML, ZEISS and TRUMPF are the partners.355 A 2018 TRUMPF article described five amplifier stages and a mean pulse power above 30 kW, against the 40 kW on the current product page.356 All that to deliver a few hundred watts of EUV; the droplet is smaller than the focus and never takes the full beam.356

On the DUV side, ASML agreed on October 17, 2012 to buy Cymer, the San Diego excimer source maker, for about EUR 1.95 billion (about US$2.6 billion) in cash and stock. Cymer had started as a four-person company that built the first excimer laser made only as a semiconductor light source. At the time its EUV sources had exposed wafers at up to 11 W at customers and reached a sustained 30 W in the lab (reported, 2012).357

Excimers also make phone screens. Excimer laser annealing (ELA) melts a thin film of amorphous silicon and lets it recrystallize as low-temperature polysilicon (LTPS), with electron mobility up to 200 times higher. The UV is absorbed in the silicon and barely reaches the glass or polyimide below, avoiding the roughly 600°C conventional processing would need. The beam is shaped into a line about as wide as the panel and scanned across mother glass, usually 1.5 x 1.85 m (Gen 6.5).358 Coherent says ELA is the only method for making LTPS for AMOLED phone displays and that its LineBeam systems are used by every major display maker - company claims - in an industry it puts at about 1.5 billion phones a year.358

Who makes industrial lasers, and how big is the business?#

Two of the best-known Western names shrank in 2024-25. IPG Photonics reported 2024 revenue of US$977.1 million, down 24% from $1,287.4 million in 2023, with materials processing at 85% of fourth-quarter revenue and fourth-quarter China sales down 22% year on year.359 It was IPG's first year under $1 billion in a decade (2022 was $1.43 billion), the year included a write-down of nearly $200 million tied to exiting Russia, and its lower-cost YLS-RI fiber laser was pitched against Chinese competition in cutting.360

TRUMPF's preliminary figures for the year to June 30, 2025 showed sales of €4.3 billion, down about 17% from €5.2 billion, orders of €4.2 billion and 17,750 employees; the CEO said "the lowest point has been reached."361 Its next fiscal-year figures put 2025/26 order intake at €4.5 billion, up 7%, and mention an EUV laser being assembled for ASML.362

Raycus, based in Wuhan's Optics Valley of China cluster of more than 200 laser companies, launched four cutting, welding and additive lasers at the CIIF fair in Shanghai in September 2025 and sells in more than 80 countries.363

The market-size figures are softer. One Chinese aggregator, citing no named sources, estimates Raycus at about 27% of China's fiber laser market, IPG's global fiber share falling from about 70% in the early 2010s to about 30-40% in 2024, and China's 2024 laser market at about RMB 50 billion.340 Grand View Research, in a paid-report press release, estimates the global laser processing market at US$42.73 billion by 2030, growing 10.0% a year from 2025, with gas lasers taking more than 65% of 2024 revenue.364

Figure Value Year Status
IPG Photonics revenue US$977.1M (-24%) 2024 Official
TRUMPF sales €4.3B (-17%) FY2024/25 Preliminary, reported
TRUMPF order intake €4.5B (+7%) FY2025/26 Official
xTool revenue RMB 2,475.9M (+70%) 2024 Reported from prospectus
China laser market about RMB 50B 2024 Estimate, unnamed sources
Global laser processing market US$42.73B by 2030 Paid-report estimate

How do factories keep multi-kilowatt lasers safe?#

Mostly by putting them in a box. OSHA has no standalone laser standard in the US; it reaches laser hazards through general-industry rules on personal protective equipment (29 CFR 1910.132) and eye and face protection (1910.133), and it points to ANSI Z136.1, the voluntary consensus standard for safe laser use, as guidance that is "not OSHA regulations."365

The most common Class 1 design is a high-power laser fully enclosed in an interlocked, labeled housing, which meets Class 1 with no extra operator controls.342 The embedded laser inside keeps its own class, so servicing with panels off may need controls for that class, and walk-in housings need a way to stop the laser running with someone inside. Many materials-processing stations use a "limited open beam path," with a gap of less than about 1/4 in under the enclosure, assessed by a Laser Safety Officer (LSO).342

The beam is only part of it. OSHA's manual says fumes and vapors from cutting and welding must stay below ACGIH threshold limit values or OSHA permissible exposure limits. It also lists lamp explosion, UV from welding plasmas, radio-frequency energy and X-rays from excimer power supplies, and warns that enclosure materials hit by more than 10 W/cm² can ignite, with fire a hazard for Class IV systems.342 Its bibliography includes studies on by-products from laser processing of Kevlar, plastics and welding, and ANSI Z136.3, the health-care standard, puts "major emphasis" on plume.342

Cue 14

Lasers in medicine and beauty

The short version: Medicine picked up the laser almost the moment it existed: a ruby laser treated a patient's retinal tumor in New York in November 1961, a year after the first laser worked. Eyes came first because the eye is a window, and a beam can reach the retina without a cut. Skin came next, once a 1983 paper showed how to heat one target - pigment, blood, ink - and leave the tissue around it alone. Today the same physics runs from LASIK and glaucoma clinics to hair removal, tattoo removal, dentistry, cancer drugs switched on by light and home caps sold for thinning hair, each with its own FDA path and its own quality of evidence.

Who first used a laser on a patient?#

On November 22, 1961, at Columbia-Presbyterian Medical Center in New York, Dr. Charles J. Campbell of the Edward S. Harkness Eye Institute destroyed a retinal tumor, an angioma, with a single ruby laser pulse lasting about a thousandth of a second.366 Dr. Charles Koester of American Optical Co., which supplied the laser, worked with him.366 The same year, Zaret and colleagues made the first laser lesions in an eye, in the iris and retina of rabbits.367 A 2011 history of eye lasers in Archives of Ophthalmology cites Campbell's clinical photocoagulation through a 1965 paper and does not mention the 1961 case; the hospital's own history leads with the 1961 patient.367366

The idea was older than the laser. In 1949 Gerhard Meyer-Schwickerath published on coagulating the retina with sunlight, then moved to carbon and xenon arc lamps.368 Photocoagulation - heating tissue with light until it seals or dies - was already an eye-clinic technique. The laser made it smaller and aimable.

Who was Leon Goldman, and why is he called the father of laser surgery?#

Leon Goldman (1905-1997) was experimenting with lasers in his research lab shortly after the first one worked in 1960.369 He performed his first laser surgery in 1966, ran the Laser Treatment Center of the Jewish Hospital in Cincinnati from 1980, and was the founding president of the American Society for Laser Medicine and Surgery.369 The Dermatology Hall of Fame calls him the "father of laser surgery".369

How did an IBM lab and a Thanksgiving turkey lead to eye surgery?#

The day after Thanksgiving in November 1981, IBM researcher Rangaswamy Srinivasan brought leftover turkey into the lab, and the team aimed an excimer laser - an ultraviolet gas laser that removes tissue in very thin layers with little heat - at cartilage still attached to the bone.370 The discovery is credited to James Wynne, Srinivasan and Samuel Blum.370 Wynne tried the laser on his own left pinky and reported no heat and no pain.370

In 1983 Trokel, Srinivasan and Braren made clean excimer incisions in corneas that had been removed from eyes.367 The first corrective laser eye surgery on a sighted person followed in March 1988.370 IBM puts the total at about 60 million people treated with laser vision correction worldwide since 1990, a company figure.370

What is the difference between PRK, LASIK and SMILE?#

All three reshape the cornea, the clear front of the eye, so it focuses differently. They differ in how the laser reaches the tissue.

Procedure What happens First FDA approval on FDA's list Laser
PRK (photorefractive keratectomy) The excimer laser reshapes the corneal surface directly, with no flap March 10, 1995: Summit Technology excimer laser system, PMA P910067371 Excimer (ultraviolet)
LASIK A thin flap is lifted, the excimer reshapes the cornea underneath, and the flap is laid back down July 30, 1998: Kremer Excimer Laser System, LaserSight Technologies, PMA P970005372 Excimer; the flap can be cut by a femtosecond laser
SMILE (small incision lenticule extraction) A femtosecond laser cuts a small disc of tissue inside the cornea, and the surgeon removes it through a small incision September 13, 2016: Zeiss VisuMax, PMA P150040371 Femtosecond

Pallikaris and colleagues coined the word "LASIK" in 1991, and in 1998 Kurtz and colleagues reported cutting corneal flaps with a femtosecond laser, one whose pulses last quadrillionths of a second.367

FDA's list of approved LASIK lasers starts on July 30, 1998, and FDA's risks page also says the first LASIK approval came in 1998.372373 IBM's history says 1997.370

The VisuMax approval covers certain nearsighted patients aged 22 and over. In its trial, 328 patients were evaluated at six months: 88% saw 20/20 or better without correction, and all but one saw 20/40 or better.374 FDA's listed complications include difficulty removing the tissue, loss of suction, debris, dry eye, and moderate to severe glare and halos.374

How many people get LASIK now?#

The most recent published US counts date from 2011, in a trade-journal article by the president of the market research firm Market Scope: about 1.4 million procedures a year in 2000 and again in 2007, falling to about 800,000 a year around 2011.375

What does FDA say about LASIK risks?#

FDA's patient page lists risks without percentages: some patients lose vision that glasses, contacts or more surgery cannot fix; glare, halos and double vision; poorer night vision; under- or over-correction; severe dry eye that may be permanent; flap problems, inflammation and infection; and worse results for very large prescriptions.373 It adds that "Long-term data are not available".373 It flags "20/20 vision or your money back" offers and package deals, and says, "There are never any guarantees in medicine."373

FDA approves the lasers, not the surgeons. "FDA does not regulate the practice of medicine and does not have a registry of doctors," its FAQ says; problems go to its MedWatch reporting system or to a state medical board.376

On July 27, 2022, FDA issued draft guidance on LASIK patient labeling. It recommends expanded labeling, a patient decision checklist handed over before surgery that covers who is a good candidate and the long-term risks, and images showing the visual symptoms that can follow.377

How common are halos and dry eye after LASIK?#

The FDA-led PROWL studies, published in JAMA Ophthalmology in January 2017, followed 534 people: active-duty US Navy personnel in PROWL-1 and civilians in PROWL-2.378 Within three months, 43% and 46% reported a visual symptom they had not had before - double images, glare, halos or starbursts.378 About 28% of people with normal dry-eye scores before surgery had mild, moderate or severe dry-eye symptoms at three months.378 At the same time, only 1-4% were dissatisfied with their vision and 1-2% with the surgery.378

The symptom figures are self-reports at three months, not a count of lasting complications, and a person can notice halos and still be glad they had the operation.

What do lasers do for the retina?#

The argon laser, discovered by Bridges in 1964, gave the retina its workhorse: L'Esperance reported a large clinical series in 1969, and Coherent began selling a slit-lamp argon photocoagulator in 1970.367 Trials set the rules for its use: Diabetic Retinopathy Study Report 8, on photocoagulation for proliferative diabetic retinopathy, in 1981, and Early Treatment Diabetic Retinopathy Study Report 3 in 1987.368 Anti-VEGF drugs injected into the eye later reduced the laser's role in wet macular degeneration.368

Is laser treatment for glaucoma as good as eye drops?#

Glaucoma lasers have a long lineage: laser iridectomy (Beckman and Sugar, 1973), argon laser trabeculoplasty (Wise and Witter's pilot, 1979) and selective laser trabeculoplasty, or SLT, introduced by Latina and colleagues in 1995.367 SLT treats the eye's drainage tissue to lower pressure.

The LiGHT trial, published June 4, 2019, randomized 718 adults with newly diagnosed open angle glaucoma or ocular hypertension at six UK hospitals to SLT first or drops first.379 After three years, 74.2% of the laser group needed no drops to stay at their target pressure.379 The trial estimated a saving of GBP 451 per patient in specialist eye care costs (2019 figure).379

What is selective photothermolysis, and what did it make possible?#

In 1983 John A. Parrish and R. Rox Anderson published selective photothermolysis in Science: choose a wavelength the target absorbs and a pulse shorter than the time it takes heat to leak out of that target, and the heat stays in the pigment, the blood vessel or the ink while the skin around it is spared.380

Target Laser When What it does
Blood vessels: port-wine stains Pulsed dye laser (yellow light) Tan's NEJM report on 35 children, 1989; on the market in the early 1990s380 Treats port-wine stains without scarring380
Hair Ruby laser 1990s (Anderson and Melanie Grossman)380 Heats the pigment in the hair follicle
Tattoo ink Q-switched lasers (nanosecond pulses) About 15 years before picosecond lasers380 Shatters ink so the body can clear it
Tattoo ink, benign pigmented lesions Picosecond laser (PicoSure) FDA-cleared by January 2, 2013381 Shorter pulses for the same job
Skin surface CO2 and erbium YAG with scanners Early 1990s380 Computer-controlled resurfacing
Skin, in a grid of columns Fractional (Fraxel) FDA-cleared in the early 2000s380 Fractional resurfacing leaves untreated skin between columns to speed healing

Dermatologist Mathew Avram calls hair removal the "most common laser treatment in medicine", an expert's view rather than a counted figure, and calls CO2 lasers "the gold standard for facial rejuvenation" that need skilled hands.380

Who is allowed to do laser hair removal?#

It depends on the state, and the rules can cover "who can own the machine, to who can develop the treatment plan, to finally, who can actually fire the laser".382 FDA regulates the laser as a device; many states regulate the person through general medical practice laws enforced by medical boards.382 A trade-association explainer sets Texas against Georgia and California to show how "requirements can vary drastically", without giving the other states' details.382

Texas runs a dedicated program, and what follows is Texas law alone. Anyone performing laser hair removal needs a certificate from the Texas Department of Licensing and Regulation, at one of four levels: Apprentice-in-Training, Technician, Senior Technician and Professional.383 Each facility needs a written contract with a consulting physician, buying a device takes a physician's order, licensed health professionals answer to their own boards, and the Department of State Health Services handles the radiation safety of the devices.383

Can laser hair removal burn you?#

The published injury figures come from lawsuits. A study of skin laser litigation from 1999 to 2012 found 175 cases, 42.9% involving non-physician operators; that share rose from 36.3% in 2008 to 77.8% in 2011, and hair removal was the most common procedure.384 In 2008-2012, 85.7% of hair removal suits involved non-physicians.384 The author, H. Ray Jalian, said unsupervised laser work is legal in many states at non-medical sites such as medical spas.384

An update covering 2012 to 2020 found 69 liability claims: 64% involved hair removal and 71% non-physician operators.385 Burns appeared in 77% of claims, scarring in 39%, pigment changes in 23% and blistering in 12%.385 The authors suspect underreporting, and they write that patient safety appears to improve when physicians, particularly dermatologists, do the work.385

These are cases that reached a court or a claim, a small and skewed slice of all treatments. Neither study gives a rate of burns per treatment.385

How are lasers used in dentistry?#

FDA's general category for surgical lasers names dentistry among the specialties where lasers cut, remove or coagulate mostly soft tissue.386 A 2019 position statement from the American Association of Endodontists says lasers were proposed for dentistry about 35 years earlier and that FDA has cleared various lasers for gum and other soft tissue work, removing decay, tooth-colored restorations and as an add-on in root canal treatment.387

The same statement is cool on the root canal claims. Laser shaping of the canal has not been shown to beat mechanical shaping, the heat can injure dentin, soft tissue and bone, and the effects of laser disinfection "on the clinical outcomes of root canal therapy are not known at this point".387

How do lasers treat cancer and brain disease?#

Photodynamic therapy (PDT) pairs a light-activated drug, a photosensitizer, with light of a matching wavelength; the drug, once lit, makes a form of oxygen that kills the cells that absorbed it.388 The light gets through only about 1 cm of tissue, so PDT is limited to tumors on or just under the skin or on the lining of organs.388 The National Cancer Institute lists FDA-approved uses for actinic keratosis, advanced cutaneous T-cell lymphoma, Barrett esophagus, basal cell skin cancer, esophageal cancer, non-small cell lung cancer and stage 0 squamous cell skin cancer, plus relief of symptoms from tumors blocking the esophagus or airway.388 Side effects include burns, swelling, pain and scarring.388

Photofrin (porfimer sodium), first approved in the US in 1995, is lit with 630 nm laser light 40 to 50 hours after injection.389 NCI says skin and eyes stay light-sensitive for about six weeks; the drug's label says to avoid sunlight and bright indoor light for at least 30 days, with sensitivity lasting "up to 90 days or more" in some patients.388389

Laser interstitial thermal therapy (LITT) works from the inside: a laser fiber placed in the brain heats and destroys tissue while MRI tracks the temperature in real time.390 Medtronic's Visualase, which uses lasers in the 800-1064 nm range, is FDA-cleared to ablate epileptic foci and brain tumors, including radiation necrosis; the maker says neurosurgeons have used it since 2007.390

Do laser hair-growth caps and red light actually work?#

Low-level light therapy (LLLT, also called photobiomodulation) uses low-power red or near-infrared light meant to stimulate tissue rather than cut it. The first FDA-cleared LLLT device for male pattern hair loss arrived in 2007, and as of January 5, 2020, 32 home-use devices were FDA-cleared and on the US market, mostly emitting red light around 650 nm (range 620-678 nm).391

The evidence is thinner than the shelf. A 2021 meta-analysis could pool only seven double-blind randomized trials, 607 people in all, found significant variation between them, saw no follow-up beyond 26 weeks and no head-to-head device comparisons, and noted that dosing was reported inconsistently; its own confidence intervals for the pooled result do not match between abstract and results.391 A 2016 review of 21 studies, only five of them randomized trials, called the cleared devices safe and effective for pattern hair loss in people who had not responded to or could not tolerate standard treatments, while noting that only one trial used intention-to-treat analysis and only one other clearly described its randomization.392

"FDA-cleared" means the device went through 510(k) as substantially equivalent to one already sold, not through the premarket approval review used for LASIK lasers. Plenty of sources say "FDA-approved" anyway, including the title of the 2021 meta-analysis.391386

Can a laser pointer damage a child's eyes?#

Case reports say yes. A 2016 series in Pediatrics described four boys aged 9 to 16 with macular injuries from laser pointers, three with possibly irreversible vision loss; the authors called for limits on children's use, education and possibly laws on sale and use.393 A six-year series from Northern Ireland, published in Eye in 2022, described 65 children (74 eyes) with macular injuries from handheld lasers.394 Their mean age was 11.6, 72% were male, and only 40% had symptoms.394 Outer retinal holes carried 3.5 times the risk of significant vision impact and damage to the external limiting membrane 3.4 times, though overall vision loss was low and more than half the eyes that arrived with impaired vision improved.394

These are case series. They describe what the injuries look like, not how often they happen. FDA's 2023 consumer update says laser pointers are not toys, that they have grown more powerful, that eye injuries "often do not cause immediate pain" and vision can fade over days, and that toys should stay within IEC Class 1; it also notes that aiming a laser at an aircraft is a federal crime.395

How does a medical laser reach the US market?#

Most surgical and cosmetic lasers fall under 21 CFR 878.4810, "laser surgical instrument for use in general and plastic surgery and in dermatology": Class II, product code GEX, reviewed by FDA's General and Plastic Surgery panel and cleared through 510(k), the route for devices shown to be substantially equivalent to one already on the market.386 The definition covers cutting, destroying, removing or coagulating mostly soft tissue across many specialties.386

Refractive lasers took the harder road. The excimer and femtosecond systems for PRK, LASIK and SMILE went through premarket approval (PMA), which requires clinical evidence of safety and effectiveness - hence PMA numbers like P910067, P970005 and P150040 on FDA's lists.371372

Cue 15

The lasers you use every day

The short version: Most of the lasers in your life are hidden and small, and they mostly measure, read or carry light rather than cut anything. A red helium-neon beam rang up the first barcode in 1974, a shrinking wavelength is what took discs from CD to Blu-ray, and glass fiber lit by lasers carries the bulk of the data that crosses oceans. The same trick of timing a light pulse runs a robotaxi's roof sensor, the depth camera in a phone and a police speed gun.

What was the first thing ever scanned with a barcode?#

A 10-pack of Wrigley's Juicy Fruit gum, priced at 67 cents, at a Marsh Supermarket in Troy, Ohio, on June 26, 1974.396 The cashier was Sharon Buchanan. The customer was Clyde Dawson, Marsh's own director of research and development, so the first shopper in barcode history was a man checking his company's homework.396 He kept the gum. It never went to the Smithsonian, though the scanner did.397

That scanner was a Spectra-Physics "model A price scanner", one of the first 10 installed at the store, wired to a computerized NCR register.397 Inside, a helium-neon laser - a gas laser with a red beam - bounced off a spinning mirror and came up through a glass plate in the counter. Light reflected off the bars hit a photodiode, a sensor that turns light into an electrical signal, and the register matched that signal against a stored product list.397

The code itself came from a committee: the Ad Hoc Committee of the Grocery Industry, organized by McKinsey & Co. in 1970.397 The spinning mirror is the part worth remembering. It sweeps one beam into a pattern of lines, so the cashier does not have to line the code up perfectly. Show lasers do the same job with galvo mirrors, a cousin of the trick explained in scanning.

Why does a Blu-ray hold more than a DVD?#

Because its laser is bluer. The whole history of the optical disc is one number going down.

Format Laser wavelength Color Capacity (format spec, as reported)
CD 780 nm near infrared about 700 MB, about 80 minutes of audio
DVD 650 nm red 4.7 GB single layer, 8.5 GB dual layer
Blu-ray 405 nm blue-violet 25 GB single layer, 50 GB dual layer
BDXL 405 nm blue-violet 100 GB (3 layers), 128 GB (4 layers)

Sources: wavelengths and CD, DVD and Blu-ray capacities398; BDXL399.

Philips and Sony agreed to develop the CD together in 1980.398 A disc stores bits as pits, tiny pressed marks that reflect light differently from the flat surface around them.398 The player's laser bounces off the spiral track, photodiodes turn the reflections into a signal, and servos keep the spot focused on the track as the disc spins.398

The limit is the spot. A lens cannot focus light into a dot much smaller than its wavelength - the diffraction limit - so a shorter wavelength reads a smaller pit, and more pits fit on the disc.399 Blu-ray pairs its 405 nm laser with a lens of numerical aperture 0.85 (how wide a cone of light the lens gathers; wider means a smaller spot) and a track pitch, the gap between neighboring turns of the spiral, of around 320 nm.399 Put 25 GB against 700 MB and a single-layer Blu-ray holds roughly 35 times what a CD does, from a laser about half the wavelength.

How does a laser printer work?#

It started as a copier with a laser bolted to the front. Gary Starkweather began working on the idea at Xerox in Webster, New York, then moved to Xerox PARC in 1971, where he built SLOT - the Scanning Laser Output Terminal - on the base of a Xerox 7000 copier.400 SLOT grew into the EARS printing system, and that same year PARC researchers showed laser printing working on a copier that turned out one page per second.400

The principle is a relay. A laser beam carries the digital page, writing it as a pattern of light, and the copier's ordinary xerographic process develops that pattern into toner on paper.400401 Xerox launched the 9700 laser printer in 1977, and it became one of the company's best-selling products.400 The IEEE now marks 1971 to 1977 as a milestone in engineering history.400

Starkweather, born January 9, 1938, held US Patent 3,970,359 and was inducted into the National Inventors Hall of Fame in 2012. He died on December 26, 2019.401

Is there really a laser in my mouse?#

In many of them, yes, and the first arrived on September 1, 2004: the Logitech MX 1000 cordless mouse, built around a laser sensor from Agilent.402 An optical mouse is a tiny camera pointed at the desk. It photographs the surface over and over and works out motion from how the texture shifts. The older kind lit the desk with an LED. The laser version lights it with a laser, which Agilent said shows surface structure an LED cannot.402

Agilent's launch numbers, all company claims from 2004: 20 times the surface tracking of an LED optical mouse, 800 counts per inch, and 5.8 megapixels per second of image processing.402 It also tracked on glossy photo paper, frosted glass and polished wood grain.402

How do lasers carry the internet across oceans?#

Through glass. In 1966 Charles Kao worked out that light could travel more than 100 km through glass fiber if the glass were pure enough. The fibers of the 1960s carried light about 20 meters.403 The first ultrapure fiber was made in 1970, and Kao shared the 2009 Nobel Prize in Physics "for groundbreaking achievements concerning the transmission of light in fibers for optical communication".403 By the Nobel committee's 2009 estimate, the world had laid over one billion km of glass fiber, enough to wrap the globe more than 25,000 times, and was adding thousands of km every hour.403

Undersea, the clever part is the amplifier. The first all-fiber transatlantic cable, TAT-8, ran in the 1980s at 1310 nm and 280 Mbit/s per fiber pair; the second generation moved to 1550 nm at 560 Mbit/s.404 Then came the erbium-doped fiber amplifier (EDFA): a few meters of fiber laced with erbium and pumped by a diode laser at 980 or 1480 nm, which boosts 1550 nm signals by about 12 to 16 dB.404 The signal stays light from one shore to the other, with no stop to be turned into electronics in the repeaters, which sit about 75 to 90 km apart.404 In 1997 TAT-12/13, APCN, Africa ONE and FLAG were being built on EDFAs at 5 Gbit/s, and WDM - several closely spaced laser colors on one fiber - was the next step to multiply capacity.404

Do undersea cables really carry 99 percent of the world's data?#

The phrase you will see is "over 99% of intercontinental data". TeleGeography, which maps the cable industry, looked into it in 2023 and answered: "We can confirm that this is true."405 It added that an exact sum is impossible without traffic data from satellite operators.405

The hard number underneath is the FCC's. Satellites carried 0.37 percent of US international capacity, in 2013 data the FCC released in 2015 (official).405 That figure covers the US only, so the global 99 percent is a well-supported estimate, and 0.37 percent is the measured slice behind it.

How does lidar work in a car or a phone?#

Lidar - light detection and ranging - fires laser pulses and times the echo. Distance is half the round-trip time multiplied by the speed of light, a method called time of flight.406 Light covers about 30 cm in a nanosecond, so the electronics count in fractions of a billionth of a second.

On a robotaxi, it is the thing on the roof. Waymo's fifth-generation Driver, announced March 4, 2020, carries a 360-degree lidar that "paints a 3D picture" of its surroundings and, by the company's 2020 figure, sees objects more than 300 meters away.407 Four short-range perimeter lidars around the sides cover tight gaps and blind spots.407

In a phone, it is a speck beside the camera lenses. Apple announced the iPhone 12 Pro and Pro Max with a LiDAR Scanner in October 2020, pitching it for augmented reality, low-light autofocus up to six times faster, and Night mode portraits.408 The emitters are VCSELs (vertical-cavity surface-emitting lasers), tiny chip lasers that shine from their top surface, in the iPhone 12 Pro, 12 Pro Max and iPad Pro, measuring depth out to about 5 meters (as reported in 2022).406 Same physics as the roof sensor, scaled from 300 meters to your living room.

Are police laser speed guns accepted in court?#

In Chicago, after a wobble, yes. In 2009 Chicago Traffic Court threw out many laser speeding tickets because the devices had not been shown to be scientifically reliable at a Frye hearing, the US hearing on whether a scientific method is generally accepted before its evidence comes in.409 On February 10, 2010, Judge Eileen Burke ruled lidar evidence admissible.409 She applied an Illinois Appellate Court (2nd District) decision upholding a DuPage County ruling, which found that lidar rests on "generally accepted scientific principles".409

That is one city's traffic court applying one appellate district's ruling, and nothing here is legal advice. The wider run of laser cases lives in cases.

What do regulators say about laser pointers and cat toys?#

"Laser cat toys" appear on the US FDA's list of laser toys as an example, though the agency gives no pet-specific guidance.395 It wants toy lasers within IEC Class 1, the lowest hazard class, carrying a "Class 1 Laser Product" label.395 It tells consumers not to buy or use a laser over 5 mW, or one that does not state its power on the label.395 It notes that laser eye injuries "often do not cause immediate pain", and that aiming a laser at an aircraft is a federal crime.395

The American Academy of Ophthalmology explains the 5 mW line. Below it, a person can usually blink or look away before injury; above it, the eye's reflexes are too slow.410 Labels are not always honest: many pointers tested ran stronger than stated, sometimes tens or hundreds of mW.410 Blue and violet beams carry extra risk because people react to them more slowly.410 In one Saudi case series covering January 2012 to January 2013, 14 males aged 11 to 30 each injured one eye; four had full-thickness macular holes, and 10 of the 14 eyes needed treatment.410

This is general information, not medical or safety advice. Why governments draw these lines, and how the classes work, is in regulation.

Cue 16

Lasers in big science: from LIGO to fusion

The short version: Some of the biggest experiments of the last 30 years run on lasers. LIGO used laser light to hear two black holes merge in 2015, the National Ignition Facility fired 192 beams to get more fusion energy out of a target than went in, and laser-cooled atoms now keep time to better than one part in 10^18. Lasers also measure the distance to the Moon, sharpen telescopes, talk to spacecraft beyond Mars and hold living cells in place.

How does LIGO detect gravitational waves with lasers?#

By splitting a laser beam and watching for a stretch smaller than a proton. Each LIGO detector is an L-shaped Michelson interferometer: a laser beam is split in two and sent down two arms, each 4 km long, where mirrors bounce the light back and forth hundreds of times before the halves meet again.411 They are set to cancel, so the photodetector at the output stays dark. A passing gravitational wave stretches one arm relative to the other, the cancellation slips, and light leaks through.411 The detectors are most sensitive from 20 to 2000 Hz.411

On September 14, 2015, at 5:51 a.m. EDT (09:51 UTC), both detectors - in Livingston, Louisiana and Hanford, Washington - caught the last fraction of a second of two black holes merging into one larger spinning black hole.412 The pair weighed 36 and 29 solar masses and sat about 1.3 billion light-years away.411 The signal climbed from 35 Hz to 150 Hz in 0.2 s, a chirp that ended in a merger.411

The size of the effect is the reason for the lasers. The strain, the fractional change in length, was 10^-21: about one proton's radius across the beams' folded path of roughly 1000 km.411 To see it, the 2010 to 2015 Advanced LIGO upgrade raised the laser power from 10 W to 200 W and swapped 11 kg mirrors for 40 kg ones.411 Weiss, Barish and Thorne shared the Nobel Prize in Physics for it, announced October 3, 2017.411

Did the National Ignition Facility produce net energy?#

At the target, yes. For the building, no. On December 5, 2022, NIF fired 2.05 MJ of laser energy and got 3.15 MJ of fusion energy back, the first ignition: a fusion shot that releases more energy than the laser delivered to the target (official LLNL figures).413 That is a target gain of about 1.5.414 Each shot also draws about 300 MJ from the grid, so 3.15 MJ is around 1 percent of what the facility used.414 The result was announced December 13, 2022, before peer review.414

The method is called indirect drive. Up to 192 laser beams fire into a hohlraum, a gold-walled cylinder about a centimeter long. The laser light turns into a bath of x-rays, which blasts the surface off a peppercorn-sized fuel capsule inside; the recoil crushes the capsule and heats the hydrogen isotopes at its center until they fuse.413 The lasers never touch the fuel.

Since then the shots have kept coming, on LLNL's official list:413

Date Fusion yield Laser energy Notes
2022-12-05 3.15 MJ 2.05 MJ first ignition
2023-07-30 3.88 MJ 2.05 MJ
2023-10-08 2.4 MJ
2023-10-30 3.4 MJ 2.2 MJ
2024-02-12 about 5.2 MJ (estimate)
2024-11-18 4.1 MJ sixth ignition
2025-02-23 5.0 MJ gain 2.44, seventh
2025-04-07 8.6 MJ (plus or minus 0.45) 2.08 MJ gain 4.13, eighth, record
2025-06-22 2.4 MJ Los Alamos-led burning-plasma shot
2025-10-01 3.5 MJ gain 1.74, tenth
2026-06-20 7.9 MJ gain about 3.8, 11th
2026-10-18 2.2 MJ gain above 1, 12th

The April 2025 record put out about four times the laser energy that went in.413

How do lasers cool atoms, and what does that have to do with clocks?#

Light carries momentum, so a stream of photons can push. Tune a laser slightly below the frequency an atom absorbs, and only atoms moving toward the beam see it shifted into resonance; they absorb, get kicked backward and slow down.415 Six beams in three opposing pairs make optical molasses, a patch of light atoms wade through as if through syrup. Add magnetic coils and you have a magneto-optical trap, first built in 1987.415

The temperatures are absurd. Sodium was cooled to about 240 µK, the Doppler limit; in 1988 Phillips reached about 40 µK through Sisyphus cooling; cesium reached about 2 µK, and helium about 0.18 µK, where atoms drift at about 2 cm/s.415 Chu, Cohen-Tannoudji and Phillips shared the 1997 Nobel Prize in Physics "for development of methods to cool and trap atoms with laser light".415 The same release pointed to Chu's atomic fountain of laser-cooled atoms as a route to clocks a hundredfold more precise.415

Those clocks arrived. In an optical lattice clock, standing waves of laser light hold about 40,000 strontium-87 atoms chilled to a few hundred nanokelvin, and a stabilized clock laser probes a very narrow optical transition.416 In 2024 Jun Ye's group at JILA and the University of Delaware published a systematic uncertainty of 8.1 x 10^-19, beating the previous NIST record of 9.4 x 10^-19.416 The best cesium microwave clocks reach about one part in 10^16; optical clocks reach at least 10^-18, and scientists want to redefine the SI second on an optical transition.416

Can you bounce a laser off the Moon?#

Yes, and the mirrors have been waiting since 1969. Apollo 11 left the first laser ranging retroreflector: a 46 cm aluminum panel holding 100 fused-silica corner cubes, each 3.8 cm across.417 A corner cube is a three-mirror prism that sends light straight back where it came from, and it needs no power. Two more reflectors went up on later Apollo missions, and a French-built one rode on the Soviet Lunokhod 2.417

The beam spreads to about a mile wide by the time it reaches the Moon, and under good conditions about one photon comes back every few seconds.417 Stations that do this include McDonald Observatory in Texas and the Observatoire de la Cote d'Azur in Grasse, France.417

NASA's own pages give two numbers for its accuracy. JPL put it at about 2 cm in 1999; NASA Goddard's eclipse site said "about 3 centimeters" in 2005.417418 They agree on the headline result: the Moon is drifting away from Earth by about 3.8 cm (1.5 in) a year.417418 Ranging has also confirmed predictions of Einstein's relativity.417

Why do observatories shine yellow lasers into the sky?#

To make a star where they need one.
